--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page is the author's preface to the work. It follows the formal censorship approval (Imprimatur) from 1801. In this section, Karl von Eckartshausen explains his motivation for writing the book, positioning it as a practical moral guide rather than a work of fanciful fiction.</meta>

# Preface.

The thought that the example of noble and philanthropic actions encourages imitation is likely the reason why so many stories have been written in our time.

However, I noticed that so few writers make the effort to draw the heroes they set up as examples from the sensory world <term>Sinnenwelt: the tangible world of physical reality and experience, as opposed to the abstract or imaginary</term>. Instead, they mostly present ideals from the world of imagination—characters that are found extremely rarely, or perhaps not at all, in human life.

Among all such educational books <term>Bildungsbücher: a genre of literature intended to cultivate the reader's character, intellect, and moral standing</term>, certainly none can be found more excellent than a certain book by an author unknown to me, titled *The Manifestation of the Spirit of Jesus on Earth* <note>original: "die Aeusserung des Geistes Jesu auf der Erde"</note>. This author chose the highest pattern of all perfection, Christ the Lord, as the model for all human circumstances and situations.

This pure idea led me to the thought that it would be more useful for the world to present matters of action and substance <note>original: "That- und Kraftsachen"; literally "deed and power-things," referring to practical, effective morality rather than mere theory</note> instead of so many fanciful <note>original: "schwärmerischer"; in this period, the term "Schwärmerei" often referred to excessive, irrational religious enthusiasm or unrealistic sentimentality</note> novels.

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that moral literature should be based on real-world examples rather than imaginary ideals, identifying the life of Christ as the ultimate practical model for human conduct.</summary>
<keywords>preface, morality, imitation, Christ as model, Bildungsbücher, practical ethics, 19th-century literature</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface. Having already established the need for practical moral examples in the previous pages, Eckartshausen now explains his specific methodology: adapting the biblical "Acts of the Apostles" into a narrative style and using the life of Christ as a foundational moral guide.</meta>

I felt that no history would be more compelling for mankind than that of the early Christians—provided it were presented in the form of narratives tailored to current tastes, and offered to individuals who are both sensitive and capable of moral growth. I therefore adapted the entire Acts of the Apostles <term>Apostelgeschichte: the fifth book of the New Testament, detailing the founding of the Christian Church and the spread of its message</term> in this manner. However, before dedicating this work to the world and my readers, I drew upon the most compelling situations of the Master of all perfection, *Christ*, among men. My goal was to demonstrate the principles of a book titled *The Spirit of Jesus*—which is little known in our region—by selecting specific passages from it.

These passages, then, which deal with the human actions of Jesus on earth and the expression of the spirit of His love, shall serve as my guiding principle. By this standard, I intend to depict all the significant relationships within the Christian community <term>Christengemeinde: the fellowship or congregation of believers in the early church</term> in several forthcoming volumes.

There is currently a widespread effort toward the moral cultivation <term>moralischer Bildung: the development of character and ethical behavior through education</term> of humanity. Could a firmer foundation be chosen for the construction of the temple of human happiness than *Christ*, the Lord Himself, who calls Himself the cornerstone that the builders rejected? <note>This refers to a biblical metaphor found in Psalm 118:22 and quoted by Jesus in the Gospels, symbolizing something initially dismissed that becomes the most essential support of a structure.</note>

---

<summary>The author explains his plan to adapt the Acts of the Apostles into a modern narrative format, using the life of Christ as the ultimate moral foundation for human happiness.</summary>
<keywords>Acts of the Apostles, Christ, moral education, Spirit of Jesus, early Christians, cornerstone, human happiness</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the author's preface to the philosophical core of the work. Having established his intent to use the life of Christ and the early Church as moral examples, Eckartshausen now begins to define the metaphysical principles—truth, order, and bliss—that underpin his worldview.</meta>

# Spirit of the Book.

---

## Man’s Sense of Truth.

The supreme need for truth exists because, without it, nothing truly good or beautiful can exist.

The world cannot provide truth because it possesses none itself; rather, it is merely an appearance <term>Erscheinung: a philosophical term referring to the world as it is perceived by the senses, rather than the underlying spiritual reality or "thing-in-itself"</term>. Truth lies beyond the world and is to be sought only in the source of order.

The will of the Deity is the bliss <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of supreme, lasting happiness and spiritual well-being</term> of created beings. This bliss, however, cannot be attained without order; for bliss itself resides within order.

Order is the will of God—the highest ideal of the world’s perfection.

Christ is the personified wisdom of a thinking God, and His humanity is the

<note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the author is likely about to describe Christ's humanity as the bridge or manifestation of this divine truth to mankind, which will continue on the next page.</note>

<vocab>Truth, Order, Bliss, Will of God, Christ, Perfection</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the book's foundational philosophy, arguing that truth and bliss are found not in the material world of appearances, but in the divine order established by God. It positions Christ as the personification of divine wisdom and the key to understanding this order.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, divine order, truth vs. appearance, bliss, will of God, personified wisdom, Christology</keywords>

most perfect will, existing under the Father's purest laws of reason. Christ, when viewed as divine-human, is Wisdom and Love.

Wisdom and Love, however, bring about order and harmony everywhere. Therefore, the results of their effects are always the Good, the True, and the Beautiful.

Christ is the Way, the Truth, and the Life <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 14:6</note>. The Way, when He is considered the highest ideal of perfection; the Truth, when we take Him as our model; the Life, when His spirit enlivens, renews, and reshapes everything within us.

He is the truth for our reason, the truth for our heart, and the truth for all our actions in human life, because He makes us truly wise, loving, just, kind, and perfect.

Christ is the model of perfection for all social ranks <note>original: "Stände" — referring to the various classes or "estates" of society, such as the clergy, nobility, and commoners</note>. His teaching is the embodiment of all human bliss <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of supreme, lasting happiness and spiritual well-being</term>.

His perfection constitutes the perfection of all social ranks. Every perfect

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page begins the main body of the text, following the Table of Contents. It introduces the first chapter, which establishes the theological foundation for the dignity of man as viewed through the lens of Jesus and the creation narrative.</meta>

### I.

## Jesus honors human nature.

---

Already in the oldest book in the world, specifically within the Mosaic account of creation <note>Referring to the Book of Genesis in the Old Testament.</note>, an honorable mention of man occurs. ”Let us make ”man, an image that shall be like ”unto us!” <note>original: "Lasset uns Menschen machen, ein Bild, das uns gleich sey!" — a translation of Genesis 1:26.</note> One may explain this expression however one wishes; a sublime idea will always lie at its foundation. Suffice it to say, the Creator has distinguished his creature, man, above all others, and has elevated him to be the ruler of the earth through **reason** <term>Vernunft: the capacity for rational thought and moral judgment</term>. According to the records of the earliest history of the human race's education, man certainly begins soon to abuse his **freedom** and to deviate from the laws of his Author. Nevertheless, the Deity still deems him worthy of special attention. He interests Himself in man's progress, condescends to instruct him, warns him against missteps, has patience with his weaknesses, and shows Himself toward him generally in the—

<column-break/>

<sig>I</sig> <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages of a gathering in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>This opening chapter argues for the inherent dignity of humanity, citing the biblical creation story where man is made in God's image and granted reason. It portrays God as a patient educator who remains invested in human progress despite the misuse of free will.</summary>

<keywords>Human Nature, Creation, Genesis, Reason, Freedom, Divine Education, Dignity, Image of God</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 2, moving from the individual patriarchs (Noah and Abraham) to the establishment of the Hebrew nation and the eventual arrival of Jesus as the culmination of divine education.</meta>

Afterward, Providence arranged it in a remarkable way so that this knowledge <note>Referring to the monotheistic knowledge of the one true God mentioned on the previous page.</note> became the inheritance of an entire nation. Their state itself is theocratic <note>A system of government in which priests rule in the name of God or a god.</note>, and they have no other ruler than their protective God. However much it changed in the future regarding external institutions, the nation never again departed from honoring the Creator of the world as their highest sovereign. It is only a pity that they honored him more through external customs than through moral conduct!

<margin>Note</margin>
Prophets zealously speak out against this, but in vain.

Finally, a Jesus appears and, according to God’s plan for all of humanity, brings about an astonishing moral revolution. This is, in brief, the course which the history of the education of the human race toward religion, and thereby toward morality, has taken.

Finding this cited here will be useful insofar as we can gather from it how worthily the Deity has treated man since the earliest times because of his moral predispositions. And does that not surely awaken joy, self-respect, and grateful love? —

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jesus's role in the "history of education" of the human race, specifically focusing on how his teachings elevated the dignity of human nature through a new moral law.</meta>

Above all, Jesus has now honored human nature very highly. Let us examine this truth more closely.

To that end, the question arises:

<margin>but rather</margin>
1) What did Jesus demand of mankind?
2) What did he do for mankind?
3) What was he himself as a man on Earth?

The first question concerns the most important part of his teaching, namely the moral law <term>Sittengesetz: the internal ethical code or "moral law" as opposed to mere external rituals</term>. Here, a few of his primary demands on mankind will be sufficient for our purpose.

> "Seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness, and all these things (physical nourishment) shall be added unto you *)" <note>original: "Trachtet am ersten nach dem Reiche Gottes..."</note>

---

*) Matthew 6:33. If one examines the entire plan of Jesus, compares his teaching with it, and especially penetrates into his spirit, then one cannot possibly doubt that by the "Kingdom of God," he envisioned something quite different from what the imagination of the coarse, sensual Jews of that time dreamed of—namely, a moral kingdom of truth and virtue.

->**Vocabulary**<-
<term>Jesus: Jesus</term>, <term>Sittengesetz: Moral law</term>, <term>Reich Gottes: Kingdom of God</term>, <term>Matth. 6. 33.: Matthew 6:33</term>, <term>Moral: Morality</term>, <term>Wahrheit: Truth</term>, <term>Tugend: Virtue</term>

<summary>This page explores how Jesus elevated human dignity by introducing a moral law centered on the "Kingdom of God," which the author defines as an internal realm of virtue rather than a physical kingdom. It sets up a three-part inquiry into Jesus's demands, actions, and personhood.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, moral law, Kingdom of God, human dignity, virtue, Matthew 6:33, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jesus's teachings, specifically expanding on the interpretation of Matthew 6:33 and the definition of the "Kingdom of God" as a moral and internal state rather than a political one.</meta>

According to this brief but all-encompassing saying of Jesus, the most important concern of mankind should be to strive for God's kingdom—or the kingdom of truth and virtue—to fulfill their moral destiny, or to become ethically good. They should allow the seed of virtue within themselves to find more and more development, striving for ever-better understanding as well as ever-purer goodness of heart. In this way, they harmonize with the will of the moral Governor of the World, actively agreeing with His recognized plan for the happiness of mankind. They should willingly pay Him homage as the most perfect Educator, collaborating in His holy daily work for the moral refinement of thinking beings, and thus honoring Him as a worthy member of His boundless kingdom. If they do this, then everything else they still need for the preservation of their bodies shall be granted to them besides.

It is only the lesser thing that God grants them through this, and it depends more on—

---

If Jesus condescended to the intellectual capacities of his contemporaries in so many cases, why not in this case! — One should apply this remark to what follows as well.

->**Vocabulary**<-
<term>Jesus: Jesus</term>, <term>God's Kingdom: Reich Gottes</term>, <term>Truth: Wahrheit</term>, <term>Virtue: Tugend</term>, <term>Moral Destiny: moralische Bestimmung</term>, <term>Governor of the World: Weltregierer</term>, <term>Human Happiness: Menschenbeseligung</term>, <term>Educator: Erzieher</term>, <term>Intellectual Capacities: Fassungskräfte</term>

<summary>The author argues that the "Kingdom of God" is an internal pursuit of virtue and moral refinement where humans act as co-workers with God. He suggests that Jesus often adapted his complex spiritual messages to the limited understanding of his audience.</summary>
<keywords>Kingdom of God, virtue, moral destiny, divine education, providence, condescension, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of the Sermon on the Mount, specifically the command to be "perfect as your Father in heaven is perfect," focusing on the inherent dignity and potential for divinity within human nature.</meta>

"...as your Father in heaven is perfect." *)

Whoever makes such a demand of human beings must indeed have high conceptions of human nature.

It is thereby presupposed that a seed of something divine <note>original: "Göttlichem"</note> lies hidden within it, which only awaits development; that it possesses the powers to perfect itself actively into the infinite, and that it is capable of soaring upward to a voluntary harmony with the Godhead. It is further presupposed that every person, through the right use of their freedom and through reason formed by religion, achieves a moral dignity that is too sublime for any irresistible compulsion to impose upon them. Rather, he should resolve out of conviction and his own choice to become a child of the Godhead—that is, to demonstrate impartial and selfless love, and to act always out of pure respect for his high destiny <note>original: "Bestimmung"; refers to the divinely intended purpose or vocation of humanity</note>. In this manner, even the Author of his life treats him with dignity, and does not violate the rights of the—

---

*) Matthew 5:45—48. <note>The author cites the conclusion of Jesus's teaching on loving one's enemies to illustrate the "impartial love" mentioned in the text.</note>

->**Vocabulary**<-
<term>Divine: Göttlichem</term>, <term>Moral Dignity: moralische Würde</term>, <term>Godhead: Gottheit</term>, <term>Religion: Religion</term>, <term>Reason: Vernunft</term>, <term>Freedom: Freyheit</term>, <term>Matthew: Matthäus</term>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the "invisible Godhead" who blesses in secret, transitioning into a poetic reflection on how nature itself mirrors this divine modesty and hidden glory.</meta>

of thanks, which are due to Her <note>The author refers to the "Godhead" or "Nature" in the feminine gender here</note> for countless benefits.

The little flower in the desert, where no human eye reaches, smells so lovely and shines so beautifully. On inaccessible mountains, nature produces so many objects of human admiration, and yet no witness is there to admire them to the praise of the Creator. The noblest forces of the soil work in silence, and the human intellect hardly knows how to fathom them. When half of our planet wraps itself in the shadows of night and all millions of people close their eyes to surrender themselves to slumber, then—as if they hardly dared to appear before a modest Omnipotence—there appear, one by one in immeasurable distance, suns beside suns, worlds upon worlds in a row. A sublime spectacle for the revelation of the greatness of God! And behold, mankind slumbers.

They still lie buried in sleep even when the radiant sun already glorifies the heavens in silent majesty and casts its golden light into the loneliest valleys.

In general, how few people, even when they are awake, are moved by the great, enchanting scenes of nature!

<meta>This page continues the previous reflection on the "modesty" of the divine and nature, specifically contrasting the indifference of the masses with the profound spiritual experience of the "wise man" who recognizes God's hidden hand in the beauty of the world.</meta>

How few have the sense and feeling for such inexpressible glory!!
How few lift their heads to the boundless vault when it smiles down so kindly in festive blue, adorned with the sun, moon, and glowing clouds! — or embrace with soul-delight the inexhaustible charms of the earth in every season! Only here and there does a wise man stand in solitude, unveiling the hidden meaning of the Godhead, contemplating the glorious exterior of the world intoxicated with joy, and finding—in quiet tears of rapture, into which his inexpressible urge for worship dissolves—the true, enviable life. But the rest of the crowd feels, receives, and enjoys without ever once remembering the invisible benefactor, much less praising Him. And what does the Godhead do? She <note>The author continues to use the feminine "Die Gottheit" (The Godhead)</note> blesses without ceasing, never tires of beautifully adorning heaven and earth, and constantly works, even in secret, for the best of her great and small children on earth.
What a pattern for man! — The highest that can be presented to him. According to Jesus's demand <note>original: "Jeſu Foderung"; referring to the biblical injunction to give alms in secret</note>, he should be like this pattern: doing good in secret, free from the desire to reap praise for it, and merely satisfied with the noble pos— <note>The word "Bes" is a catchword for the next page, likely "Besitz" (possession) or "Bewusstsein" (consciousness/awareness)</note>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the previous discussion on the dignity of man, moving from the practice of secret charity to the necessity and nature of private prayer as taught by Jesus.</meta>

Prayer is a necessity for man because he feels an impulse toward devotion, senses his dependence on the highest Being, requires the communication of secret matters of the heart for his own peace, and gains many a noble satisfaction for his moral nature thereby. But, according to Jesus's demand, he should turn directly to the source—that is, occupy himself with the Deity in silent prayer, without a slave-like mentality <note>original: "Sclavensinn," referring to a spirit of fearful or forced obedience</note>, without the outward appearance of piety, without a mediator or intercessor—but rather as a pupil of heaven, as his own priest and consecrated one, as a friend or child of the house. Between him and the Deity there is nothing—what dignity for man! Was not Jesus just as acquainted with our noblest needs as he was familiar with our innermost stirrings, in that he guided us precisely to the source of all comfort, all peace, all bliss? Full of respect for our nature, he taught us only to bend the knee before the Deity, and demanded of the true worshiper of God that in secret prayer there be only a childlike mind, only humility and trust, but not slavish degradation, groveling flattery, and the usury of "good works" <term>werkheiligen Wucher: the hypocritical idea of "earning" divine favor through performative religious acts</term>. — Blessed be this man by every friend of reason and

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 12 regarding Jesus’s teachings on prayer, specifically focusing on the direct relationship between the individual and the Divine without the need for intermediaries.</meta>

human dignity, in that he proclaimed the grace of heaven even to the lowliest petitioner from a pure heart, without the intercession of a favorite! If only we had never strayed from his demand, which is so honorable to human nature! But it is precisely because of this that men have fallen into two equally uncertain detours. A large portion, who find it not at all necessary to become more intimate with a moral Deity through prayer, prefer to rely defiantly on their own strengths; they seek peace in vain upon the surging sea of stormy desires, have a sense only for the worthless trifles of childish vanity, and, with an increasingly dulled feeling for all that is noble, great, and divine, eventually lose themselves in the morass of a shameful irreligiosity. Powerful must be the lever that is to bring these people forth again, sunken deep like heavy masses. Another portion, no less blinded, throws itself slavishly down before lifeless images, worships wood and stones, practices usury with prayers <note>original: "treibt Wucher mit Gebeten," referring to the repetitive, transactional counting of prayers to "buy" divine favor</note>, and in this manner completely denies the dignity of human nature. A lack of instruction has led these blinded souls astray, so that with their reason darkened <note>original: "verfinsterter Vernunft"</note>, they are not in a position to feel shame for their miserable idolatry <term>Götzendienst: the worship of false idols or the substitution of ritual for true spiritual connection</term>.

But enough of the demands of Jesus upon mankind. Those cited so far sufficiently explain what high concepts he had of human nature.

Why, then, should one lose sight of or push aside this very important point of view? Why should that which Jesus demands of men not come preeminently into consideration when the nobility and dignity of humanity are discussed? It proves, after all, first and foremost what man can achieve and what he is meant to become through the right use of his moral powers. --- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on moral dignity from page 25, transitioning from the individual's inner struggle against vice to the broader concept of the "Kingdom of God" as a moral community independent of the state.</meta>

I cannot yet close this important observation without drawing attention to several equally important conclusions. One should examine whether they are appropriate to the spirit of Jesus. There is a Kingdom of God on earth, a kingdom of truth and virtue, entirely independent of state institutions and external force.

In this realm, no distinction of civil classes or privileges applies. The condition for becoming a member of this kingdom is moral goodness, promoted, nourished, and strengthened by religion. Now, it cannot be denied that the moral law commands and obligates man for its own sake. However, according to experience, the diversity of people and their spiritual needs is very great. Most still require a special divine authority in order to respect the command of the moral law within themselves as the voice of God.

For this purpose, the religion of Jesus is in the world. Moreover, love for God, who gave us humans such a high destiny, and love for Jesus, who has so highly honored our nature, must act united upon our hearts and [strengthen] our power in the struggle against <term>sensuality<note>German: "Sinnlichkeit." Refers to the physical impulses and desires that compete with one's moral and rational will.</note></term>—

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the equality of souls in death to the universal revelation of God through the natural world, positioning nature as a primary "temple" accessible to all humanity.</meta>

### 2.
## Jesus in the Open Temple of Nature.

---

The oldest revelation of God is and remains nature. A person would have to be narrow-minded and devoid of feeling for all things divine to deny this. Here, in this temple, the human spirit perceives the true, universal language of its God. Nowhere do His dispositions reveal themselves more amiably than here. Nowhere do such great, comforting, and universally comprehensible truths press themselves upon the intellect and the heart as they do here. Anyone who walks in this temple with even a little reflection must soon become aware of the duty to embrace with tenderness, forbearance, and love all those in whom God takes such great interest through all the works and effects of nature. Thus, the entire immeasurable heaven, with its blue garment and the friendly, radiant sun, is certainly not the throne of a God who condemns human beings on account of their weaknesses.

<sig>3</sig> <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to keep the folded sheets of a book in the correct order.</note>

<vocab>Revelation, Nature, God, Temple, Heaven, Humanity, Creation</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that nature serves as God's first and most universal revelation, teaching a message of divine love and human compassion that transcends religious dogma. He suggests that the beauty of the physical world reflects a God who is forgiving of human frailty rather than one who seeks to condemn.</summary>
<keywords>natural theology, divine revelation, nature as temple, compassion, universalism, God's love, creation</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section, moving from the general concept of nature as a temple to the specific emotional and moral effects that observing the natural world has on the human spirit.</meta>

Universal mercy, which every human being requires in equal measure, reveals itself constantly in nature as God’s most beautiful name. Countless beings proclaim to us here, near and far, in their joyful voices, that we cannot be indifferent to the Spirit of the World, who provides so graciously for even the lesser creatures. Everything in nature is divinely beautiful, everything is great, solemn, and sublime; and through this very fact, the Creator has proven how our spirit is meant to be formed by the immediate objects surrounding us. The beautiful is intended to move the spirit, to enchant it, and to elevate it toward moral beauty. The great, the solemn, and the sublime are intended to impart the mood necessary to soar above the wretched trifles of lowly worries, wishes, and desires. Where does the heart expand so greatly as in the vastness of creation? Where do we, children of moral freedom, breathe as freely as in her free domain? And the circle of our noble joys—how uncommonly large it becomes here, where every flower enchants us, every blade of grass charms us, every dewdrop refreshes us, and every purple cloud delights us, if only we have the sense for it! Nowhere, however, do we feel our human dignity <term>Menschenwürde: a central Enlightenment concept referring to the inherent, inviolable worth of every person</term> so much as in the temple of nature. Everything reminds

He emphasizes that the vastness of creation provides a unique space for experiencing true moral freedom and noble joy.</summary>
<keywords>universal mercy, moral beauty, human dignity, sublime, creation, spiritual elevation, Enlightenment thought</keywords>

must have been the most comprehensible way for them to explain the lonely withdrawal of their teacher, who was so sensitive to nature.

Oh! To look up there with a heart of the highest moral beauty, declared the favorite of the Godhead by this very heart, to lose oneself among a thousand times a thousand worlds of the majestic heavens—that nothingness...

Assertion: Jesus was a silent observer of nature; he filled his spirit with sublime ideas in this temple, explored the secret meaning beneath the veil, and knew, as it were, no better way to bring religion closer to the hearts of men than in connection with the wonders of the visible creation. But now, let us also hear the divine Sage himself, as he interprets the universally comprehensible, comforting, touching, and powerful language of God in nature.

> “Look at the birds under the heaven. They do not sow, they do not reap, they do not gather into barns, and yet your heavenly Father feeds them. Are you not much more than they? — Look at the lilies in the field, how they grow. They do not labor, nor do they spin.

> I tell you, that even King Solomon in all his love of splendor <note>original: "Prachtliebe" - a fondness for magnificence or luxury</note> was not clothed as gloriously as such a flower. — Should not God, who so clothes the grass of the field, which stands today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven; should he not do that much more for you, O you of little faith” *)!

ble temple, and allowed the intellect to find the seeds of wisdom there, and the heart to feel the power of truth even there, where the wonders of God were close to the senses. That is why what he taught had such an irresistibly immediate effect on people. Thus, we can now boldly assert: whoever diminishes nature; whoever is not moved by her great, solemn, and sublime effects; whoever passes coldly by the glories of heaven and earth, hardly glancing at them, let alone worshipping the Deity for them; whoever remains indifferent to everything that nature teaches him, and never seeks, recognizes, admires, and loves the Father of all beings with reverence in His creation—that person is not a human being according to the spirit of Jesus. Rather, to seek, find, praise, and worship God even there, where Jesus sought, found, praised, and worshipped Him, means to harmonize with his spirit in this regard as well.

## 3.
# Jesus in His Hometown.

---

It seems to be a hereditary disease of the soul, which has been propagated from generation to generation, to determine the value of a person only according to outward appearances. The first question upon the sight of a stranger is always this: who is that? By this, one merely wishes to learn the person's social standing <term>Stand: refers to one's social class, rank, or station in life, which was strictly defined in the 19th century</term>. Depending on whether the standing is declared high or low, the judgment is also tuned higher or lower and the degree of respect is measured out. One turns up their nose with contempt when the clothing and the exterior have announced a man of rank, and yet the information gathered does not agree with it. It was already so millennia ago.

Jesus also once visited the synagogue in his hometown of Nazareth and delivered an emphatic speech before his countrymen *).

*) Matthew 13:54. Luke 4:16—30. Mark 6:1—6. <note>These biblical citations refer to the accounts of Jesus being rejected by the people of Nazareth who knew him only as the carpenter's son.</note>

<meta>This page continues the reflection on Jesus's rejection in Nazareth, moving from the critique of social status to an exhortation on how to educate children through the "book of nature" to avoid such narrow-mindedness.</meta>

your children — accustom them from youth upward to the quiet contemplation of nature and make this for them the first school of religion.

Burden their understanding with nothing, and let nothing be burdened upon it, through which the sanctuary of visible creation is profaned and the eternally valid teachings of God in this sanctuary are contradicted. Children and adults still desire, just as in Jesus's time, to have the great, comprehensible, glorious divine language of nature <note>original: "Gottessprache der Natur"</note> interpreted into human language in order to arrive at living knowledge and conviction. Do this then according to the spirit of the wisest of men — for surely you would not wish to understand the matter better than he — and hope thereby to work irresistibly upon the hearts of your children — children for eternity!!

### 3.

## Jesus in His Home Town.

---

It seems to be a hereditary disease of the soul, propagated from generation to generation, to determine the value of a person only according to outward appearances. The first question upon the sight of a stranger is always this: "Who is that?" By this, one merely wishes to learn their social standing <note>original: "Stand" — referring to one's rank, class, or position in the social hierarchy</note>. Depending on whether that standing is pronounced high or low, the judgment is also tuned higher or lower, and the degree of respect is measured out accordingly. One turns up their nose with contempt when the clothing and the exterior have announced a man of rank, and yet the information gathered does not correspond with it. So it was already thousands of years ago.

Jesus also once visited the synagogue in his home town of Nazareth and delivered an emphatic speech before his countrymen *).

---

*) Matthew 13:54; Luke 4:16–30; Mark 6:1–6.

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jesus's visit to Nazareth, moving from the general observation of human prejudice to the specific reactions of the townspeople as recorded in the Gospels.</meta>

People are generally amazed by his great wisdom, since he was, after all, raised in Nazareth. A rational person would perhaps have judged in this way:

> “This man has received great talents, and it redounds to his honor that he has developed them so beautifully despite his lowly birth. His wisdom deserves our respect, his heart our reverence. What he says is good, what he teaches is true. One must follow him.”

— But the citizens of Nazareth do not judge in this way.

Soon this one asks, then that one:

> “Is this not the carpenter, Joseph’s son? Is his mother’s name not Mary? — Are James and Joseph, Jude and Simon not his brothers? Are his sisters not also here among us? — From where, then, does this man have such great wisdom and the power to do such deeds?”

— One sees immediately what a sad prejudice has taken hold of their minds. In their opinion, the carpenter’s son—despite all his wisdom and virtue—is an insignificant person who does not deserve to be admired. A magnificent outward appearance, splendor and pomp for the senses, a wealthy retinue in one's service, and similar deceptive appearances <note>original: "Trugerscheinungen" — illusions or phantasms that mislead the eye</note>

<vocab>Nazareth, wisdom, judged, Joseph's son, Mary, James, deeds, prejudice, minds/souls, deceptive appearances</vocab>

<summary>The text illustrates the rejection of Jesus by his neighbors in Nazareth, who allowed their familiarity with his humble origins and family to blind them to his wisdom and works.</summary>
<keywords>Nazareth, Jesus, carpenter's son, prejudice, social status, wisdom, family of Jesus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of Jesus's rejection in Nazareth, transitioning from the townspeople's initial skepticism based on his humble origins to their violent physical reaction against him.</meta>

would certainly have produced a completely different effect. Now, however, people everywhere take offense at the outward appearance and the social circumstances of Jesus, and are prejudiced against his person, failing to revere the wise man in the son of the carpenter, or the friend and teacher of virtue in the member of a lowly family. But soon that sorrowful prejudice also transforms into hatred and bitterness. Jesus recommends better dispositions to the citizens of Nazareth if they wish to be worthy of the kingdom of God. This is unbearable to them. What person does not wish to dream on in his moral slumber and remain undisturbed in his self-love? — Against all right and fairness, they drive him out of their city and even attempt to hurl him down from a nearby mountain height.

True frenzy <note>original: "Raserey" — a state of violent agitation or madness</note>, into which the faults of the heart finally degenerate when they find no resistance! — Now let everyone place themselves in thought in the position of Jesus. — How the insult would have grieved him and the poor treatment cast him down! How his courage would have sunk, and his entire peace would have vanished! — But Je-

The ungrateful Nazareth wonders at the blindness of the people, and Jesus remains true to himself in his quiet greatness, in his sublime peace of soul.

How little his zeal to teach the truth was diminished by this adverse incident is shown immediately on the spot; for he goes into the villages round about and teaches*). The injustice he suffered must certainly have pained him deeply, and he surely felt it all the more strongly the more refined and moral his human sensibilities were, and the more love urged him to do good everywhere and to promote the improvement of the heart.

What admiration, therefore, his unshakable virtue and the firmness of his character deserve, that he did not allow himself to be wearied in his activity, intimidated in his zeal, or held back in his course even by such poor treatment! ### 4.
## Jesus, a Friend of Children.

When the mature trees of a forest stand withered and mournful at the time when they should be turning green, and upon investigation one finds the cause to be some incurable damage that has let them fade and die away: is there then any hope of ever seeing this area of the forest again in the revitalizing charm of spring? — Only a single means remains to replace the loss. Young saplings must be planted and preserved through protection so that they gain height and strength, and in time serve as an ornament to the entire forest. — In the same way, children are the nurseries <note>The author uses the term "Pflanzschulen" (plant-schools/nurseries) as a metaphor for the educational environment where young minds are cultivated.</note> through which the moral kingdom of God on earth can most readily be populated, even if little hope remains for the older generation.

From children, one can expect much, provided that protection, maintenance, and care are applied

<summary>Using the metaphor of a forest being replanted with young saplings, the author argues that children represent the best hope for establishing a moral kingdom of God when the older generation has become spiritually withered.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, children, education, moral kingdom of God, renewal, metaphor, youth, cultivation</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 58, moving from the metaphor of young saplings in a forest to the specific biblical account of Jesus welcoming children as the ideal candidates for the kingdom of God.</meta>

become, and expect a new, better human race. Thus, even one who despairs of his own age still finds comfort, courage, and hope when he directs his attention toward children. How amiable must Jesus now appear to us as the bosom friend of all children! Once, parents brought their little ones to him so that he, according to the common custom as a recognized prophet, might lay his hands upon them and bless them*). Undoubtedly, the press of people was very great at that time. Therefore, his disciples held the parents back, so as not to disturb or annoy the teacher—who was overwhelmed with more important business—with such a trifle. But the parents would not allow themselves to be held back. Jesus usually noticed all individual incidents around him with sharp observation. All the less could the behavior of his disciples remain hidden from him. Affably, he began: "Let the little children come to me, and do not hinder them, for they are precisely the most fit for the kingdom of God. Truly I say to you: whoever does not receive the kingdom of God as a little child will not enter into it." — And as he

He understood perfectly how to interact with people, how to influence parents through their children (an important maxim for all teachers), and how to use every innocent national custom as nourishment for love. — 

-> "Truly, I say to you: whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a little child will never enter it." <-
<note>original: "Wahrlich, ich sage euch: wer nicht das Reich Gottes empfähet als ein Kindlein, der wird nicht hineinkommen." (Mark 10:15 / Luke 18:17)</note>

May these words from the mouth of Jesus be dear and holy to all parents, all teachers, and all adults! — You who read them here, at least have such good intentions for posterity that you never neglect your children through deformity <note>The author likely refers to "moral deformity" or the warping of a child's character through poor upbringing.</note>, nor seduce them through immoral examples! With anxious love, tend to the moral germ <term>moralischer Keim: the innate potential for goodness and virtue within a child</term> in their hearts, so that it is not stifled, but rather develops without harm, grows without disturbance, blooms, ripens, and in the end brings forth the most magnificent fruit.

If the natural inclinations which the Creator implanted in you, and the feelings of humanity, and the voice of reason are not yet strong enough to move your heart to this: then learn at least from God’s messenger, the founder of your religion, what duty toward children demands of you.

<meta>This page continues the exhortation to parents, building on the previous page's discussion of Jesus's love for children as a model for moral education and the rejection of worldly ambition.</meta>

Since He, out of pure love, blessed them as those most fit for the Kingdom of God, what must you do, when even nature binds you more closely to them and the foundation for their moral well-being can rightfully be demanded of you? —

Good parents! Never look upon your children without thinking that Jesus loved children so heartily as precious plants for God’s kingdom.

Then, honestly tend to the plants entrusted to you, so that they grow up to be an ornament in the kingdom of truth and virtue. —

Once, a dispute also arose among the disciples of Jesus as to which of them was the most prominent? It is well known from what prejudice this dispute originated. Each one wanted to have the greatest advantages in the glittering kingdom which, according to Jewish hopes <note>The author refers to the contemporary Jewish expectation of a political Messiah who would liberate Israel from Roman rule and establish a worldly empire.</note>, the Messiah was to establish on earth. I would remind you here of the weaknesses of the human heart, if He, who was without weaknesses, did not immediately reconcile us to it through a beautiful act of His life. The dispute over rank among His disciples gave Him another opportunity to reveal the most amiable affection toward children. —

### 5.

### Jesus, slandered by his enemies.

To love the truth with an honest heart and to profess it with boldness <note>original: "Freymüthigkeit" (frankness or courage of speech)</note> deserves applause, esteem, and admiration. Nevertheless, it is not always so in the world. Truth does not always find applause. All too soon it comes into collision with the inclinations and passions of men. All too soon it offends against prejudices, habits, and prevailing sentiments which stubbornly resist it. It also finds itself, all too soon, struggling against errors, imaginings, and follies that would rather remain concealed than be called by their right names. What then is the consequence? — Perverted and blinded men do not wish to give the truth a hearing and want to suppress it. But since they are not in a position to harm the truth in itself, nor to contradict it with any basis in reason, they seek at least to make the bold confessor of the same appear suspicious to—

<vocab>Jesus, truth, boldness, applause, slander, prejudices, passions</vocab>

<summary>The author discusses how the honest pursuit and profession of truth often meet with resistance and slander from those whose prejudices and passions are challenged by it.</summary>
<keywords>truth, boldness, prejudice, slander, Jesus, opposition, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 68 regarding the inevitable backlash faced by those who speak the truth, transitioning from general observations to the specific historical example of Jesus.</meta>

make. This is the most common origin of a slander.

All great men of antiquity have since been slandered by their contemporaries as friends and admirers of truth.

It is no wonder, then, that Jesus also saw himself exposed to these poisonous arrows. He loved the truth and proclaimed it publicly, because it was his calling to gain followers for it. Thus, it was so often said among the embittered Jews: "He misleads the people: a) shall Christ come out of Galilee? b) are you also misled? Does any ruler of the Pharisees believe in him? c) He bears witness of himself, his testimony is not true; d) you are a Samaritan and have a devil; e) the man is not from God, because he does not keep the Sabbath; f) we know that this man is a sinner; g) He has a devil and is insane, why do you listen to him? h) This—" <note>The text breaks off here, continuing the list of accusations in the following section.</note>

### 6.

## Jesus, when he praises and is praised.

Usually, a virtuous person feels a lively joy regarding everything that harmonizes with their own dominant character. Could a person like Jesus have remained undistinguished in this regard? — When he once entered Capernaum, a centurion <term>Centurion: a professional officer in the Roman army, typically in command of 100 soldiers.</term> approached him and said: "Lord! In my house a servant <note>The original German says "Soldat" (soldier), though the biblical source usually refers to a servant or slave.</note> lies suffering from paralysis <note>original: "Gicht" — while this literally means gout today, in the 18th and 19th centuries it often referred to palsy or paralysis, matching the biblical account in Matthew 8.</note>, and is in great pain." <a> The readiness to help, which Jesus immediately expressed with the words: "I will come and make him well," — was entirely worthy of this brave man, who, moved by the suffering of his subordinate, sought active help in every way. Soon, this same man showed another beautiful side of his heart. Modesty did not allow him to trouble the willing friend of humanity with many

...and Jacob in the kingdom of heaven.” The names of those long deceased already teach us that he was pointing toward a future world—to a kingdom of truth and virtue beyond the boundaries of the earth. 

God, how our feelings are lifted, how our spirit expands at the thought: to be gathered there with all the noble, good, and wise people of all ages and all nations, and to find imperishable bliss in their company, in their love, and in their friendship! There I shall find you, noble *Socrates!* as well as you, immortal *Gellert!* <note>Christian Fürchtegott Gellert (1715–1769) was a famous German Enlightenment poet and moralist, often held up as a model of Christian character alongside the ancient wisdom of Socrates.</note> united with all the other great souls, venerable through wisdom, religion, and virtue, who once cultivated their character as human beings on earth. Who would not gladly weaken the power of sensuality <note>original: "Sinnlichkeit" — in this context, it refers to the physical or worldly impulses that can distract one from a moral or spiritual life.</note> and pay homage to virtue, in order to become worthy of such company?

...loving mother <note>The text begins mid-sentence: "[zärt]liche Mutter," completing the thought from the previous page.</note>, when her beloved child suffers? Tenderness and love drive her to try everything. Lord! said the Canaanite woman, and she herself fell down before him:

> “Lord, have mercy on me and help me!”

Jesus answered:

> “It is not right to take the children’s bread and throw it to the dogs.”

A great appearance of harshness, which however does not frighten away the tenderly worried mother, but only reveals her noble confidence. And that was precisely the intention of the most benevolent friend of humanity <note>original: "Menschenfreund" — a term often used in Enlightenment-era theology to describe Jesus’s character as one who loves and seeks the best for all of mankind.</note>. The Gentiles <note>original: "Heiden" — non-Jewish people, who were often viewed as outsiders to the divine covenant.</note> were always despised by the Jews and regarded as outcasts. Jesus now wanted to test the woman's sentiments more closely and thereby put the Jews to shame. However, in no case did he have the intention of belittling the good woman along with her nation. He would have greatly offended the maternal feeling with which she pleaded, and instead of the pity which her sad situation deserved, he would have treated her cruelly. A Jesus was not capable of that. The Gentile woman did not believe it either.

> “Lord,” she said: “yet even the little dogs eat the crumbs that fall from their master's table.”

— Then Jesus cried out, full of wonder:

> O woman! your faith is great: let it happen for you as you wish!

Johannes the Baptist <note>John the Baptist, the prophet who prepared the way for Jesus's ministry.</note> sent some disciples from the prison where he sat as a confessor of the truth <note>original: "Bekenner der Wahrheit" — John was imprisoned by Herod Antipas for speaking out against Herod’s unlawful marriage.</note> to Jesus, and had them ask him:

From this varied behavior, several important conclusions can be drawn, and one should examine whether they are in accordance with the spirit of Jesus. — The virtuous sage <note>German: "tugendhafte Weise." A common Enlightenment-era term for an idealized person who lives according to reason and high moral principles.</note> must consider human judgments from his own point of view when they concern his moral worth. Insofar as private inclinations, passions, and self-serving intentions play the greatest part in such judgments, they must be a matter of indifference to him.

They can mean even less to him when he considers that differing ways of thinking, short-sightedness, and a restricted view into the inner self <note>The author suggests that outsiders cannot truly see a person's soul or motivations.</note> rarely allow for judgment according to the truth. Often, even praise and blame are abused in a shameful way when they are intended to characterize a person. How can the virtuous sage determine the degree of his self-respect based on human judgments? Rather, he has an inner judge <note>A reference to the conscience, often described in 18th-century philosophy as a "tribunal" within the mind.</note> who blames or praises him, and whose judgment alone is impartial, and consequently the only one valid for him. ### 7.

## ->Jesus Great in a Small Family Scene.<-

---

There were two sisters in a house where Jesus often used to visit, both gentle and good-natured, but both very different in their temperament as well as in their taste. Mary only felt great pleasure when she could satisfy her thirst for knowledge <note>The German *Wißbegierde* refers to a profound desire to learn or intellectual curiosity.</note> and hear wisdom from the mouth of Jesus. Enriching her spirit with knowledge remained always her primary occupation. Martha, her sister, was more interested in the household, and even when she could have been learning and hearing Jesus, she did not let herself be kept from her womanly duties <note>The text uses *weiblichen Verrichtungen*, referring to the traditional domestic chores expected of women in the 18th and 19th centuries.</note>. Moreover, Jesus’s friendship itself guarantees that both individuals, despite all their different directions of activity, deserved high esteem in regards to their hearts. And is the heart not always the main thing that decides the value of the whole person? Jesus also visits

once in the home of this good family. <margin>Luke 10:38–42 <note>original: "Luc. 10, 38—42."</note></margin> Martha shows herself once more in her true character; that is, she zealously makes preparations to decently entertain the beloved guest. The author uses this word, which usually means a culinary "dish," to pun on the "better part" Mary chose, contrasting it with the literal meal Martha was preparing.</note>, which shall not be taken from her.” — With such gentle consideration did the wise friend of humanity <note>A common Enlightenment-era title for Jesus, emphasizing his kindness and love for all people.</note> treat both sisters. To reproach the diligent hostess, to reproach her without qualification, would have been harsh. After all, it was for his sake that she occupied herself with such great care, and it was done from a good heart. Only the excess of business or the anxious worry, along with the persistent unrest, was she to avoid, and of this she was now lovingly reminded. Only out of consideration for himself did the sensitive guest let her feel that she was taking too much trouble, saying: “what I need for my entertainment is but little.” — But should Mary then be blamed? She acted indeed out of an eagerness to learn, as she listened to the beloved teacher. The pious feelings of her heart harmonized with the teachings of truth from his mouth. To nourish her spirit through his conversation was indeed her best meal, and perhaps the guest spoke directly to her, specifically according to her concerns and needs.

> “Mary has chosen a good dish <note>original: "Gericht." The author continues the wordplay from the previous page, where "Gericht" can mean a culinary dish or a "portion." It refers to the "better part" (Luke 10:42) Mary chose.</note>, which must not be taken from her.” // —

Few words, but very telling. It is my food, Jesus once said, that I do the will of God, that I spread the truth, and instruct people for their refinement <term>Veredlung: A key Enlightenment concept referring to the moral and spiritual improvement of a person's character.</term>. If we compare those words with these, it was actually Mary who fed him according to his own heart; for she gave him the opportunity to do the will of God and allowed herself to be instructed for her further refinement. At the same time, she herself partook in this meal; her spirit was nourished through the conversation with the teacher. This is the good dish that Jesus meant—a dish of which those certainly have no conception who are accustomed to thinking and living only sensually <note>original: "sinnlich." In this context, it refers to focusing exclusively on physical needs and material comforts rather than intellectual or spiritual growth.</note>. Who does not also admire, in this small family mat—

### 8.
## Jesus should not and will not condemn.

First to let the accusers, filled with shame, fall silent and withdraw, and then to acquit the repentant defendant so that they might improve themselves: that is a noble deed of wisdom and philanthropy <note>The original German *Menschenliebe* (literally "love for humanity") was a core virtue in Enlightenment-era thought, signifying a universal compassion and desire for the well-being of others.</note>. Jesus performed such a deed. a) — Once, while he was teaching in the temple, behold, the legal scholars <term>Gesetzausleger: Literally "interpreters of the law," referring to the scribes or experts in Mosaic religious law.</term> and Pharisees brought to him a woman caught in adultery and said: “Moses commanded us to stone such a person. What do you say to this?” — The cunning ones, how they wish to catch the friend of humanity by surprise and put him in an embarrassing position! They are already too well convinced by his heart that he would not condemn the woman: but this very fact is intended to serve as proof for them that he despises the precepts of Moses. In this way, the devious ones intend to trap the noble man through his very love for humanity. —

a) John 8:1 <note>While the citation notes verse 1, the narrative of the woman caught in adultery technically begins at John 8:3.</note>

<meta>This page continues the reflection on Jesus’s encounter with the woman caught in adultery, following the conclusion of the biblical narrative on the previous page. It transitions from the story itself to a philosophical analysis of Jesus’s character and his victory over the Pharisees.</meta>

Prudence, guided by wisdom, triumphs over low cunning; so much does a sound understanding, united with goodness of heart, outweigh all the schemes of vice. Jesus could not have acted in a manner more worthy of himself than by leading the accusers back to their own hearts. They, the greater sinners, wanted a transgression punished according to the very law that condemned them themselves in terms of morality—a situation that occurs not infrequently among judges. At that moment, it was said from the mouth of the Wisest: “Whoever among you is without such sin, let him throw the first stone.” <note>A reference to John 8:7. The author emphasizes that the "sin" Jesus refers to is the same type of moral failing the accusers were currently judging.</note>

With their guilty consciences, they could not bear this predicament. It was a dangerous internal enemy that robbed them of all courage and composure, forcing them to take flight on the spot to avoid revealing the secret shame of their hearts through visible embarrassment. Yet, it is not only the wisdom, the prudence, and the deep insight into human nature <term>Menschenkenntniß: The intuitive ability to perceive the inner character, motives, and psychological state of others.</term> of Jesus that I admire here, but also his moral goodness, even toward his enemies.

His pronouncement was intended to simultaneously awaken their moral feeling and give them the opportunity for self-reflection. What a spirit, who in that very same out<note>The word "Ausspruch" (pronouncement/statement) is interrupted by the page break.</note>

<summary>This page analyzes Jesus’s psychological and moral victory over the accusers of the woman caught in adultery. The author argues that by forcing the accusers to confront their own consciences, Jesus displayed a perfect balance of intellectual prudence and moral goodness.</summary>

moment, when he is being hostilely pursued and dangerous snares are being set for him, he not only renders the pursuit harmless for himself, and not only escapes the snares without danger, but also takes pity on those who are hostile and brings their own inner corruption <note>original: "innern Schaden." This refers to the spiritual or moral decay of the soul caused by hypocrisy.</note> to their attention!

What a spirit, who with those very same words acquits the guilty woman—who recognizes herself as guilty—and at the same time frankly censures the guilty men—who consider themselves guiltless—in order to improve them both!

To unite and achieve so many noble, great purposes at once, like the Divinity, was something Jesus could do; and moreover, in a matter where his cunning enemies had devised the evil plan to catch him off guard and put him in a state of embarrassment.

Oh, if only his spirit would animate all people! #### 9.
### Jesus in His condescension toward the weak.
<note>original: "Herablassung." In 18th-century theology, this term refers to "divine accommodation"—the idea that a higher being (like Jesus) graciously lowers himself to the level of human understanding to teach effectively, rather than acting in a patronizing way.</note>

Who is the truly great man in terms of intellect <term>Verstand: The faculty of the mind used for logic and reasoning.</term> and heart? — Certainly not he who, with the weight of his superior insights, becomes a burden or even harmful to the weak by ruthlessly tearing down and rejecting everything upon which their conviction is based. Nor is it he who looks down upon the simple-minded with complacency, pride, and contempt, and considers it worth neither the effort nor his reputation to teach them better.

On the other hand, to be helpful even to the weakest in reaching the knowledge of truth despite all superiority of insight; to connect with their first concepts and feelings; to correct their common judgments; to utilize their conviction for their practical life; and to make the approval of their reason effective for their moral disposition—

<vocab>Jesus, condescension, intellect, heart, insights, the weak, conviction, knowledge, truth, reason, moral disposition</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a reflection on Jesus's intellectual and moral greatness, defining a "great man" not as someone who overpowers others with knowledge, but as someone who meets the weak at their level to help them grow. It highlights Jesus’s method of teaching through empathy and the gradual correction of common understanding.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, condescension, intellectual humility, education, reason, moral disposition, Enlightenment theology</keywords>

at the very moment when he is hostilely pursued and dangerous snares <note>original: "Fallstricke." A common 18th-century metaphor for the logical or legal traps set by the Pharisees to force Jesus into a contradiction of the Law.</note> are laid for him, he does more than just render the pursuit harmless and escape the traps without danger; he even takes pity on his enemies and warns them of the harm they do to their own souls!

What a spirit! With the very same words, he pardons the guilty woman—who acknowledges her fault—while simultaneously and boldly rebuking those guilty men who consider themselves innocent, all for the purpose of improving them both!

To unite and achieve so many noble, great, and truly divine <note>original: "gleich der Gottheit," meaning "in the manner of the Godhead."</note> purposes all at once—this was something only Jesus could do, even in a situation where his cunning enemies had devised a wicked plan to trap him through surprise and embarrassment.

Oh, if only his spirit would animate all people! If only all laws of statecraft <term>Politik: In this context, it refers more to the philosophy of governance and law-making than modern party politics.</term> were written in this spirit!—for experience shows that many a guilty person is punished in such a way that the penalty either makes their improvement entirely impossible or delays it even further.

### 9.
## Jesus in His Condescension toward the Weak. <note>original: "Herablassung." In this historical theological context, this term refers to "divine accommodation"—the act of a superior being graciously lowering themselves to the level of others to help them, rather than the modern negative sense of being "patronizing."</note>

Who is the truly great man in terms of both intellect and heart? — Certainly not he who, through the overwhelming weight of his superior insights, becomes burdensome or even harmful to the weak by ruthlessly tearing down and rejecting everything upon which their convictions are founded. Nor is it he who looks down upon the simple <note>original: "Einfältigen." In 18th-century German, this often referred to the "simple-hearted" or uneducated common folk with a sense of pastoral sympathy rather than modern mockery.</note> with self-complacency, pride, and contempt, and considers it worth neither the effort nor his reputation to better instruct them.

On the other hand, to be helpful even to the weakest in recognizing the truth—despite all superiority in insights—to connect with their initial concepts and feelings, to correct their common judgments, to utilize their convictions for their practical life, and to make the approval of their reason effective for their moral disposition <note>The text ends mid-sentence here: "wirksam ma-" is almost certainly a truncation of "wirksam machen," meaning "to make effective."</note>

<summary>This page defines true greatness not as intellectual superiority, but as the ability to meet the "weak" or uneducated at their own level to lead them toward truth. It presents Jesus’s humble "condescension" as the ultimate model for teachers and leaders.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, condescension, intellectual humility, moral disposition, the weak, divine accommodation, education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological reflection on the Parable of the Unforgiving Servant. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the obligation to be merciful to others while being recipients of God's mercy ourselves.</meta>

->- 104 -<-

—to be [merciful], as an object of divine mercy despite one's own, often greater, faults: all this becomes immediately clear to anyone who takes to heart the fate of that rich and poor man <note>The Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus from the Gospel of Luke, chapter 16.</note>, the deed of that Samaritan, that story of the prodigal son, and the behavior of the king toward his subjects. It is the same with all the other stories of Jesus. Their material is borrowed entirely from common life.

Even a weak understanding can grasp and comprehend the truth hidden within them. This truth simultaneously becomes immensely interesting to the heart, imprints itself more deeply upon the soul <note>German: *Gemüthe*; a term referring to the combined emotional and spiritual nature of a person.</note>, and promotes further reflection. What shall I say of the grace <note>German: *Anmuth*; referring to the aesthetic and spiritual beauty of the narratives.</note> by which each story is uniquely distinguished, or of their inner power, which moves as much as it refreshes?

And when Jesus thus condescended to the intellectual capacity of the weak, he always had a moral purpose in view. The weak were to be instructed, made acquainted with their moral duties, made familiar with the mind of God, won over for religion, and [led] to their own—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 104 regarding Jesus's teaching methods. The previous page ended mid-word with the prefix "eig-", which completes here as "eigenen innern Veredlung" (their own inner refinement).</meta>

->- 105 -<-

—own inner refinement. 

Another method of Jesus's wise condescension <note>In this context, "condescension" (German: *Herablassung*) is a theological term for "accommodation," where a teacher simplifies complex divine truths so they can be understood by ordinary people.</note> consisted in the fact that he attached important truths to visible, everyday phenomena in both nature and domestic life. He placed these truths in connection with those daily sights and made them vivid by means of striking comparisons. In this regard, he did not look down upon even the smallest objects; indeed, the more often such things were used in common life, the more firmly he linked a moral lesson to them.

> "The kingdom of heaven is like a mustard seed, which a man took and sowed in his field." f) — "The kingdom of heaven is like leaven, which a woman took and mixed into three measures <term>Scheffel; an old European unit of dry volume used for grain or flour, similar to a bushel</term> of flour." g)

> "The kingdom of heaven is like a precious pearl, found by a merchant who sold everything he had and bought the pearl." h)

> "The kingdom of heaven is like a net thrown into the sea, which catches every kind of fish." i)

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jesus's pedagogical methods, specifically how he linked abstract spiritual concepts to the physical world to make them more memorable for his audience.</meta>

...the fourth observation <note>This refers to a previous section of the text where the author likely categorized Jesus’s teaching methods.</note> mentioned there. — Nothing could be more comprehensible and convincing for the common people <note>original: *schwache Volk*; literally "weak people," likely referring to the uneducated masses or those "weak" in theological understanding</note> than this manner of instruction.

The supernatural <term>Uebersinnliche; literally "over-sensory," referring to spiritual or divine realities that cannot be seen or touched</term>, when made tangible through sensory objects, worked all the more vividly upon the heart and, by means of the association of ideas <note>A psychological concept where one thought triggers another; here, a physical object triggers a spiritual lesson.</note>, was renewed all the more frequently in the soul <note>original: *Gemüthe*; a term encompassing the mind, heart, and emotional temperament</note>. It is well enough known how easily a person—when prompted to think of something remarkable, true, religious, and moral in connection with visible, everyday phenomena—remembers it again as often as they perceive those same phenomena. Moreover, from the image—presented especially by charming nature or the interesting domestic circle—something attractive, pleasant, and refreshing always reflects back upon the truth linked to it. For which person does the all-enlivening sun in the sky have no charm?

Who is not moved by the sight of an innocent flock of sheep? Whose heart is not refreshed by wine? Who does not feel the need for salt at a meal, or for a drink when thirsty? In this way, the most sublime teacher <note>A common honorific for Jesus used in 18th and 19th-century German theological writing.</note> understood...

[he] chose: was that not handled most simply and, at the same time, most wisely? — Now, of course, times have changed. What for those students <note>original: *Schüler*; while usually translated as "disciples" in a religious context, the German term literally means "students" or "pupils," emphasizing Jesus’s role as a teacher</note> was still merely an object of faith has for us already become an object of experience.

The world history of eighteen centuries <note>This reference suggests the text was written in the late 18th or early 19th century, looking back at the time since the founding of Christianity.</note> teaches us what Providence <term>Vorsehung; the protective care of God as a spiritual power guiding human destiny</term> has done for the spread of religion, and we see the course of its arrangements before our eyes.

God’s plan is fully known to us through the events which have occurred since that time. Indeed, we know by what means the obstacles, which at first seemed insurmountable, were successfully overcome. In short, for us, the future has been transformed into the past, and we stand at a vantage point from which we can look back and see what has transpired in the course of things. But the apostles were in a completely different situation. For them, it was undoubtedly a specific need of their time to rely with unshakeable faith on the cooperation of the Ruler of the World <term>Weltregierer; a contemporary theological term for God as the supreme governor of the universe</term>, and thus to remain faithful to their apostolic office <term>Apostelamt; the mission and duties assigned to the apostles</term> under all difficult circumstances. At times, Jesus also adapted himself to an accommodating method <term>herablassende Methode; in a pedagogical sense, this refers to a teacher "stooping" or lowering themselves to the level of the student's current understanding to better lead them to the truth</term>, in which he did not directly refute a false judgment, but instead drew a conclusion from it. Through this, the falsehood of the judgment was brought to light, and the truth was acknowledged with conviction. For example, the Pharisees once claimed that he performed his miracles only through the power of the prince of devils. Jesus answered:

> "Every kingdom that is divided against itself goes to ruin, and one house falls over the other. If Satan is also divided against himself, how will his kingdom stand? — When a strong armed man guards his palace, there remains..."

“Who among you, intending to build a tower <note>original: "Thurm"; here used to illustrate the spiritual foresight needed to follow a path to completion.</note>, does not first sit down and count the cost, whether he has enough to complete it? “Which of you, having a servant <note>original: "Knecht"; referring to a bondservant or laborer in a domestic and agricultural context.</note> who is plowing or tending sheep, will say to him when he comes in from the field, ‘Go at once and sit down at the table’? > "General truths should be **clothed** <note>original: "eingekleidet"; a common Enlightenment pedagogical concept where abstract ideas are "dressed up" in stories or parables to make them easier to understand</note> for the uninstructed, and presented through individual cases in a way that is comprehensible, convincing, and practical."

To grasp the abstract and the **supersensible** <term>Uebersinnliche: that which exists beyond the reach of the five senses, such as spiritual, metaphysical, or highly theoretical concepts</term> requires a great deal of exercise of the intellect. Therefore, if the uninstructed is also to advance in his **knowledge**, if he is to improve himself **morally**: then he must learn vividly from individual cases what is right and what is duty. This will have an incomparably greater effect on him than the most sublime formula of **moral philosophy** <term>Sittenlehre: the formal system of ethics or the study of moral conduct</term>.

> "One should not want to improve everything at once; for that is in vain; and even less should one demand perfection immediately: instead, even in the **moral refinement** of the uninstructed, one should follow the step-by-step, gradual path of God in nature."

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the gradual nature of moral improvement from page 126. It elaborates on the necessity of patience and gentle guidance when dealing with the prejudices and superstitions of those who are not yet fully enlightened, using the historical example of Jesus as a pedagogical model.</meta>

- 127 -

Prejudices, superstitions, diverse customs, and strange practices are found everywhere in human society. In such cases, it is a duty to spare that which must remain for the time being, because of its inseparable connection with much that is good. It is a duty to meet the weak on their own level <note>original: "herabzulassen"; in the 18th-century context, this did not mean to be patronizing, but rather to "descend" or adapt one's level of speech and expectation to match the listener's current understanding</note>, so as not to tear away the most necessary supports of their **morality** <term>Moralität: the internal quality of a person's character and their adherence to ethical principles</term>; a duty to quietly and imperceptibly prepare their minds for something better, and for the time being only to purify the old ways of the harmful elements that hinder and delay progress. This is the task of the wise **philanthropist** <term>Menschenfreund: literally "friend of humanity"; a central ideal of the Enlightenment, describing an individual dedicated to the welfare, education, and moral progress of others</term>, who possesses the sense and feeling for the great divine work of laboring successfully for the moral welfare of humanity.

Thus acted a man like Jesus, and through the wisdom of this approach, his religion has spread ever further as a blessing to the world. May we continue to build upon this foundation, and in time, if not complete the moral edifice, at least make it ever more perfect.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jesus as a pedagogical model from page 127.
