### 10.
## Jesus in His Regard for Freedom.

---

It is impossible to hold human nature in honor, and to love it above all as the noblest thing on earth, without sparing the **freedom** <note>original: "Freyheit"; in this context, it refers to moral agency and the capacity for self-determination</note>—which God has made the distinguishing characteristic of human beings—with a tender heart. In this respect as well, Jesus appears great and amiable. Even the undertaking itself—to redeem humanity from the **slavery of sin** <term>Sklaverey der Sünde: a common theological and philosophical metaphor describing the state of being controlled by one's vices, irrational impulses, or baser instincts rather than by reason</term> and to elevate them to the divine freedom of a spirit that knows no other dominion than that which truth and right command, and no other submission than that which the eternal laws of virtue require—deserves our full admiration. Indeed, no person can truly be called free unless he uses his freedom correctly, or, with the agreement of his will, sanctifies all his powers, drives, and **passions** <term>Affekten: in 18th-century philosophy, these are strong emotional states or "affects" that must be governed by reason to achieve moral perfection</term> toward the ultimate goal of refining his being more and more, and attaining the highest possible degree in-

<meta>catchword: in-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces a discussion on Jesus's deep respect for human freedom, defining true liberty not as license, but as the voluntary alignment of one's will with the laws of virtue and truth.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, freedom, human nature, slavery of sin, divine freedom, virtue, passions, moral agency, Enlightenment theology</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jesus as a model for human development. The previous page ended mid-word with the prefix "in-," which is completed here as "innerer" (inner).</meta>

inner perfection; then he, the truly worthy human being, stands at the peak of visible nature, harmonizing with the rest of God’s creatures—who all, through their physical organization or instinct, necessarily become what they are meant to be according to their **destiny** <note>original: "Bestimmung"; this refers to the teleological purpose or "vocation" of a human being in 18th-century philosophy</note>. Thus, he need not be put to shame by any tree of the field, any flower of the meadow, or any little worm in the grass. 

Therefore, the dominion of **vice** is so dishonoring to the human being, because it degrades him below his destiny, placing him in contradiction with himself and with all objects of living and inanimate creation; in general, it causes him to strive against the intentions of the almighty, all-wise, and all-beneficent Godhead. 

But Jesus did not only seek to destroy this dominion of vice over humanity: it was also his constant effort to take human freedom under his protection against the **slavery of prejudices, errors, and arbitrary religious services** <note>original: "willkührlicher Gottesdienste"; this refers to religious rituals performed out of habit or obligation rather than genuine spiritual conviction</note>.

He was always zealous in his opposition to the **Pharisees** <note>a Jewish sect at the time of Jesus known for strict adherence to traditional and written law; the author views them here as symbols of restrictive traditionalism</note> of his time, because they shackled the free spirit of humanity in this manner.

> “They bind heavy, unbearable burdens, and lay them on people’s shoulders, without...” <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 23:4, where Jesus criticizes religious leaders for imposing legalistic requirements on others</note>

<summary>This page concludes the argument that human dignity relies on moral freedom and explains how Jesus sought to liberate the human spirit from both the internal "slavery" of vice and the external "slavery" of empty religious legalism.</summary>
<keywords>inner perfection, human destiny, vice, freedom, Pharisees, religious ritual, moral agency</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page completes the biblical quotation from Matthew 23 begun on the previous page and continues the author's argument that Jesus sought to liberate humanity from the "slavery of prejudice."</meta>

...to touch them with so much as a single finger.” (a) — Let them go; they are blind, and leaders of the blind. But if one blind person leads another, both will fall into the pit. (b) “Come to me, all you who are weary and burdened, and I will give you rest. My yoke is easy, and my burden is light.” (c) — (The precepts that I give you are grounded in your own nature. What I teach you will do you good, make you divinely free, and bless you eternally.)

It was this very behavior that laid the first foundation for the irreconcilable hatred with which those moral slaves persecuted him throughout his life. Truly, one must heartily love and highly esteem the man who thought and judged in such a way, if only for the sake of the universal interest that Right, Duty, Truth, and Virtue find in him!

— May this principle convince and guide all hearts! Truth can indeed be sought and found on various paths. If you have chosen this path for yourself, friend! then do not bear any ill will toward another person, much less hinder him, if he takes a path that—according to his own conviction—brings him more effectively to the same destination you reach by your own. Promoting the good is and remains the primary purpose in the world everywhere; but there are, again, quite different means to that end. --- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument from the previous page, moving from the individual’s choice of path to a broader philosophical justification for diversity based on the nature of God and the physical world.</meta>

All moral people on earth agree that they love truth, promote the good, and strive to contribute their share to the welfare of the world. But in the manner and way in which they conduct themselves in this regard, uniformity rarely occurs, nor is it intended to occur according to God's will. He himself reveals himself to us everywhere as a lover of diversity <note>original: "Liebhaber des Mannigfaltigen"</note>, and wherever we look, His visible world proclaims the most beautiful harmony amidst the greatest variety. Should it be any different in the moral world? — By no means.

Whoever, therefore, works solely for the promotion of the good is for, and not against, the good, even if his path or his means differ from other paths and means; he is for, and not against, the moral law <term>Sittengesetz: the objective ethical principles that govern human conduct, often associated in this period with the innate conscience or "categorical imperative"</term>, even if he belongs to an entirely foreign party <note>original: "Parthey"; referring to a religious sect or philosophical school</note>. Thus, human freedom of spirit remains in its dignity, and here too, the greatest harmony unites with the highest diversity according to the will of God.

## II.

# Jesus Under False Judgment.

---

Is it not common for some people to permit themselves the most flagrant excesses, yet slander the truly noble man—who never knowingly allows himself to deviate from his duties—at every opportunity, simply because he does not conform to their strange customs and long-standing <note>original: "verjährten" - meaning entrenched or prescriptive prejudices that have persisted over many years</note> prejudices? This fate Jesus shared with so many noble souls. If the earth were not, according to God’s plan, a school of virtue and a training ground for higher perfection, our hearts might be revolted when we see how the honest, active, and wise worshiper of God so often bleeds under the scourge of worthless people. —

A man with a withered <note>original: "gelähmten" - paralyzed or crippled</note> hand was brought to Jesus precisely on the Sabbath <term>Sabbath: the traditional Jewish day of rest; the act of healing on this day was often used by critics to accuse Jesus of breaking religious law</term>. a)

---

a) Luke 6:6. <note>original: "Luc. 6, 6."</note>

It frames the world as a "school of virtue" where the noble are often tested by the prejudices of others, specifically referencing the healing of the man with the withered hand.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, Sabbath, prejudice, Luke 6:6, moral perfection, judgment of Jesus, religious law</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jesus facing opposition from religious authorities. Following the introduction of the man with the withered hand on the previous page, the text now details the confrontation with the Pharisees regarding healing on the Sabbath and transitions into other instances of Jesus challenging social and religious norms, such as the healing of a paralyzed man and dining with tax collectors.</meta>

The interpreters of the law and Pharisees lurked on all sides, watching to see if he would help the suffering man on the holy day. What a sad display of hardness, apathy, and heartlessness! To save a life or to destroy it?" With these words, he looked at each of them in turn, and since no one answered him, he called out to the infirm man: "Stretch out your hand!" He did so and was healed. — In exactly the same manner, a man paralyzed by gout <note>original: "Gicht" — while modernly referring to a specific joint disease, in this period it often described various forms of paralysis or crippling limb pain</note> was healed. b) "Your sins are forgiven you," Jesus said: "Arise! ### 12.

## Jesus, a Great Teacher in Small Matters.

---

There is an extraordinary amount of instruction to be found in the visible creation and in human life for those who have accustomed themselves to notice everything with presence of mind <note>Original: "Geistesgegenwart"—this implies a state of being mentally alert and spiritually receptive to one's surroundings.</note> and to observe all things around them. There is no place where such a person could not ponder the most sublime truths and find material for the most fruitful reflections. Even trifles <note>The author uses "Kleinigkeiten" to refer to the small, seemingly insignificant details of everyday existence that others might overlook.</note> then become important, and it will always remain the mark of a true sage when he seeks to increase his insights even in such small matters, to sharpen his spirit of observation, to elevate his intellect, and then, by sharing his beneficial results, seeks to be of service to others as well. 

This is how Jesus acted. Many a person had perhaps already seen how the wealthy made offerings to the treasury <term>Gotteskasten: literally "God’s box," referring to the thirteen trumpet-shaped collection chests in the Temple treasury where voluntary offerings were deposited.</term> at the Temple, and thought nothing of it. The matter was something so ordinary and

### 13.

## Jesus as a Guest at the Table.

---

One must also observe a person at a festive meal, in respectable and joyful company. There, he often forgets himself and reveals his true character. In such moments, social customs, the prevailing tone, and sensual pleasures <note>original: "sinnliche Wollust"; in this context, it refers to the indulgence of the senses through food, drink, and luxury rather than modern sexual connotations.</note> usually carry him away to such an extent that he hardly remains conscious of his own self. He encounters such strong temptations—at times toward intemperance and gluttony, at others toward pretense and flattery, and sometimes toward slavish parroting <term>Nachbeten (slavish parroting): To mindlessly repeat the opinions or words of others in order to fit in or please the crowd.</term> and imitation—that only the firmest principles can withstand them. 

If a wise man remains true to himself even in such glittering company and at an open banquet, appearing no different in his mind <note>original: "Kopf"; literally "head," referring here to his intellect or rational judgment.</note> and heart than he does at any other time, then Wisdom calls him her true favorite, and Virtue calls him her worthy devotee. Jesus was such a man. A leader of the Pharisees <term>Oberster der Pharisäer (leader of the Pharisees): A high-ranking member of the Jewish sect known for their strict and public adherence to religious law.</term> invited him on the

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jesus at the Pharisee's dinner. Having observed the guests' behavior on the previous page, the text now records Jesus's specific teaching on humility and social conduct, followed by a moral commentary on his pedagogical style.</meta>

“When you,” he says, “are invited by someone to a great feast, do not take the place of honor. It may be that a more distinguished person than you has been invited, and then the host will come and say to you: ‘Give your place to this man!’ and you must take the lowest seat with shame. Rather, when you are invited as a guest, go and sit at the very bottom. If the host then comes and says to you: ‘Friend! Move up higher!’—then you will be honored before the whole company sitting at the table with you. For whoever humbles himself will be exalted; and whoever exalts himself will be humbled.” <note>This is a paraphrase of the Parable of the Guests found in the Gospel of Luke 14:8–11.</note>

— Candor, profound understanding, a fine sense of propriety <note>original: "Schicklichen"; referring to what is socially appropriate or decorous</note>, love of truth, and goodness of heart reveal themselves here united in the remarkable words of Jesus. Pharisaic pride is not spared, but instead, the sense of honor <note>original: "Ehrgefühl"; a person's internal compass for dignity and social standing</note> is directed toward a better path; a beautiful proof of how he never wished to wound, much less root out, the fundamental impulses of the human heart, but only to guide them properly and win them over for the benefit of virtue. And is not modest humility still the surest means of attaining honor?

Who, according to experience, is more honored: the proud man, who seeks advantages in trifles, or the humble man, who modestly steps back? The former constantly comes into collision with the self-love <note>original: "Eigenliebe"; refers here to the ego or self-interest of others which reacts poorly to arrogance</note> and the primary inclinations of other people, and is therefore despised or put to shame. The latter is valued, loved, and preferred precisely because of his humility, and it is considered an honor to voluntarily show him honor. —

Now Jesus turns with equal frankness and love of truth to the host and speaks to him:

> “When you prepare a meal in the future, at noon or in the evening, do not invite your friends, relatives, brothers, and neighbors who are rich—with the intention of being invited by them in return, and thereby finding repayment <note>original: "Vergeltung"; here referring to the social cycle of reciprocal invitations common in the ancient world</note>. Rather, invite to your meal the poor, the crippled, the lame, and the blind. You will be blessed then, as these wretched ones cannot repay you. Only in the kingdom of true merit will repayment for this be granted to you.” <note>This is a paraphrase of Luke 14:12–14.</note>

<vocab>the proud, the humble, self-love, Jesus, host, meal, the poor, the crippled, the lame, the blind, repayment</vocab>

<summary>Jesus advises his host to abandon the social custom of inviting only those who can reciprocate, urging him instead to show charity to the marginalized. The text emphasizes that true honor and spiritual reward come from acts of kindness that cannot be repaid on earth.</summary>

A meal given by **Virtue** refreshes both body and soul, and when **Love** feeds her guests, then hearts celebrate a feast of joy. 

On no occasion, however, can a wealthy man prove his good disposition more, practice his virtue more, or strengthen his love more than when he hosts the poor, the wretched, and the needy. Every bite enjoyed in happiness, every refreshing drink, every cheerful face, every beaming eye, every tear of joy, and every grateful expression...

### 14.
## Jesus in Conflict with Deceit.

---

<margin>a) Luke 20:20–26; Matthew 22:15–22.</margin>

There is no more excellent spectacle in the **moral world** <note>The author uses "moral world" to describe the sphere of human behavior and ethics as a cosmic stage where good and evil interact</note> than when **deceit** <note>original: "Arglist"; referring to a specific type of malicious cunning</note> employs all its arts to lure the man of divine wisdom into a trap, and he then puts all those applied arts to shame through a single, simple, and unaffected statement. — The Pharisees resolve to outwit Jesus—known as a friend of truth—through the very **virtue** <term>Tugend|In this context, it refers to Jesus's perceived honesty and integrity, which the Pharisees hope to weaponize against him</term> that they hypocritically praise in him. They approach and address him thus: “You always speak the truth freely and judge without regard for the person. Tell us then, is it right for us to pay **tribute** <term>Zins|Refers to the census tax or head tax imposed by the Roman Empire on its subjects</term> to the Roman Emperor or not?” — a) A very treacherous question! According to their wish, it mattered little whether he affirmed or denied it. In both cases, he is—

trapped. If he answers in the affirmative, the people will be incensed, because nothing is more abominable to them than to pay **taxes** <term>Abgaben|General term for taxes or duties</term> to a pagan authority, and because, according to general expectation, the **Messiah** <term>Messias|The expected liberator of the Jewish people</term> is supposed to put an end to these oppressions. Let us examine Jesus’s answer. Could it have been more striking or wiser? His enemies are not in a position to reproach him: that he is an opponent of the Emperor and is inciting the restless people against Roman sovereignty. ## 15.
### Jesus is kind, even when he refuses a request.

---

Being unable to refuse anything, even where higher duties require it, is a great weakness. It is often called good-naturedness <note>original: "Gutmüthigkeit"; in this context, it refers to a specific type of moral compliancy or people-pleasing that the author views as a character flaw rather than a virtue</note>; but it deserves reproach. Such "good-natured" people usually promise what they cannot perform, and would rather become unfaithful to their word than frankly excuse themselves from a request. They thus commit a real fault in order to avoid an imaginary one, and, against their own will, cause great harm.

What if others relied entirely upon their promise? What if these people later found themselves deceived, falling into anxiety, grief, and vexation because of it? And are there not cunning people from all walks of life who abuse this "good-naturedness" and seek to use it to achieve goals which they ought, by duty, to achieve through the application of their own efforts? Many an idler would be forced to work, and in the sweat <note>The text likely continues "of his brow," referencing the biblical requirement for human labor.</note>

<vocab>Jesus, good-naturedness, weakness, duties, idler, abuse</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that the inability to say "no" is a moral failing rather than a virtue, as it leads to broken promises and allows others to avoid their own responsibilities. The author contrasts this weak "good-naturedness" with the purposeful kindness of Jesus, which can include necessary refusals.</summary>
<keywords>good-naturedness, moral weakness, reliability, social duty, idleness, integrity, Christian ethics</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 170 regarding the dangers of "good-naturedness" (Gutmüthigkeit). It completes the biblical allusion to "the sweat of one's brow" and transitions into a theological reflection on how Jesus provides a model for setting moral boundaries.</meta>

of his brow to earn his bread, if he did not rely on the support of the good-natured! "These people can refuse me nothing," he thinks; he becomes pushy in his demands, resorts to pretense <note>original: "Verstellung"; this refers to the act of feigning a certain character or motive to manipulate others</note>, and is further emboldened in his immoral behavior every time he succeeds in reaching his goal through such unethical means. Under these circumstances, good-naturedness is an unpardonable weakness and even a significant flaw.

But what if someone demands things from us that our conscience does not approve of, and which we cannot agree to without debasing our moral dignity? Should we still be unable to refuse? In that case, it is a sacred duty to frankly reject any such demand, and virtue itself sanctifies <note>hallows or makes holy</note> such a refusal. Let us now also learn the truth from Jesus: that one can be very kind even when refusing a request.

To be sure, Jesus always showed a proactive willingness to serve and to help whenever he was asked for instruction <note>original: "Belehrung"</note> or for deliverance from some evil; and even if he did seem to hesitate in such a case,

### 16.

## Jesus upon the Provocation to Revenge.

---

<margin>*) Luke 9:51–59.</margin>
Who in the world was more elevated above all revenge than Jesus? — He was just about to travel to Jerusalem and take his way through the Samaritan territory when the Samaritans, biased by hatred against the Jewish people, refused him passage and lodging. This occurred in a **border village** <term>Gränzflecken</term> <note>original: "Gränzflecken"; an archaic term for a small settlement or market town located on a border.</note>. As soon as the messengers sent by Jesus returned with such unpleasant news, John and James began:

> “Lord! If you wish, we will command fire to descend from heaven and consume them, just as **Elijah** <note>A reference to the prophet Elijah in the Old Testament (2 Kings 1), who called down fire to consume soldiers sent to arrest him.</note> once did?”

One should not wonder at all at the language of the two disciples. It was merely a natural human impulse to which they felt inclined in that moment.

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jesus's response to the Samaritans' rejection, shifting from the disciples' vengeful reaction to an explanation of Jesus's empathy and his understanding of the historical and social roots of the Samaritans' hostility.</meta>

On no occasion is a person more easily moved to anger than when their sense of honor is attacked; and nowhere does the desire for revenge burn more strongly than when the respect due to one’s person is ignored.

Only Jesus, ever full of mercy toward the blinded and full of forbearance—like the Deity toward those who err—did not allow himself to be provoked in the slightest by the perverse behavior of the Samaritans. He also knew best the source of this misguided conduct. From the Jewish side, the Samaritans were despised and insulted. People did not even observe toward them those duties which humanity demands. Was it any wonder that they, in turn, despised the Jews and treated them as declared enemies on every occasion? — Yet had they known Jesus properly, as the friend of all mankind, and especially as their own friend—who in his parables always presented the Samaritan in a more beautiful light than the Jew b): they certainly would have received him with joy. Consequently, their behavior stemmed from ignorance, and because

Nowhere do these passions stir sooner or more violently than when one’s **sense of honor** is wounded.

Behold here the most beautiful proof of how the spirit of a teacher can eventually pass into the spirit of his student. ## 17.

# Jesus at the Grave of the Friend.

---

The one who so kindly comforted that foreign widow as she accompanied her only son to the grave, with the address: "do not weep!" <note>original: "weine nicht!"; This refers to the Raising of the Son of the Widow of Nain in Luke 7:13.</note> — and who instantly removed the cause of her tears by returning her son to her: he learned while away that his friend Lazarus had died. Martha and Mary, the two sisters of the deceased, now wept inconsolably over the loss, and they were friends of Jesus.

Often he had been hosted in their home and had always found a warm reception. What else could the sorrowful family expect but that their virtuous, loving friend would come in haste and comfort them? But they likely did not imagine that their tears of misery <note>original: "Jammerthränen"; literally "tears of lamentation" or "wretchedness."</note> over the loss of their brother would so soon be transformed into tears of joy by the brother being returned to them. The great Jesus had likely erred in his opinion this time, if he claimed that the sickness would not lead to death. At the very least, people thought, he had not wanted to come and help his sick friend. It certainly remained a contradiction that Jesus so often helped strangers, and yet now did not want to help his friend. But when do weak humans ever judge based on rational consideration as soon as appearances align so little with hope? Neither the disciples of Jesus nor the grieving family suspected anything of the great event that was soon to take place before their eyes. quieted the heart. “Our friend Lazarus is sleeping!” Has anything ever been said more briefly, more touchingly, more nobly, and in a more pleasant image? The friend, for whom one wishes all good things and for whom one is concerned because of his illness, lies in gentle sleep—in a state where he feels no pain, no torment, no restlessness, and at the same time offers every hope for a speedy recovery. Under this charming image, the imagination had to be prepared, as it were, to let the repulsiveness of death vanish from the mind. In this way, one can also be a **friend of humanity** <note>The German term "Menschenfreund" (philanthropist) was a high ideal during the Enlightenment, denoting someone who acts out of pure love for fellow humans.</note> in one’s expressions, and demonstrate heartfelt love through carefully chosen words. Finally, here too, Jesus spoke of **God’s glory** <note>original: "Gottes Ehre"</note>. One cannot summarize his entire **character** more briefly than by saying: everything that related to him, his fate, his effectiveness, the outside world, the present, and the future—he considered, judged, utilized, tolerated, and endured it all with constant regard for the **Deity**; or rather, he lived, thought, and acted with an ever pure...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Jesus’s internal state, transitioning from his constant orientation toward the divine to the practical application of this mindset in the biblical narrative of Lazarus’s death.</meta>

### Consciousness through and for the Deity.

Does it not, in the whole of **human life**, depend mainly on the prevailing **mood** of our heart? According to this mood, everything in the world takes on its color.

If we only ensure that this mood is **morally-religious**, then we live and move <note>A likely allusion to Acts 17:28: "For in him we live and move and have our being."</note> everywhere in the element of **morality** and **religiosity**, and then indeed the true **spirit of Jesus** animates us.

Soon the group approaches the village of **Bethany** <note>Bethany was a small village on the Mount of Olives, about two miles from Jerusalem.</note>. Immediately the rumor spreads that Jesus is on his way. **Martha**, in keeping with her well-known lively temperament <note>Martha is traditionally depicted in the Gospels as the more active, pragmatic sister, while Mary is seen as more contemplative.</note>, cannot wait for the arrival of her friend. She rushes out to meet him and says: “Lord! If you had been here, my brother would not have died.” The other sister, **Mary**, says exactly the same when she arrives with her entire retinue of mourners.

Jesus assures them that the deceased shall **rise again**; and so that this might not be interpreted—according to the **Jewish faith**—as referring only to the general resurrection <note>The author refers to the contemporary Jewish belief, held particularly by the Pharisees, that the dead would be raised together at the end of days.</note>, he adds quite

So it is with the human heart. When it is besieged by the sensations of a passion, the intellect rarely has the strength to engage in reflection. Twice his eyes overflow with tears before the tomb of the deceased Lazarus. Tears do not dishonor a wise man, for they are the highest expression of mixed emotions, and say in a single moment more than a thousand words might say. And are there not tears of a nobler kind, which have already flowed upon God’s earth, and still constantly flow? The benefactor weeps them because he cannot help as he wills and heartily wishes. The religious person weeps them over the weakness of his virtue and over the power of sensuality <term>sensuality: in this context, the German *Sinnlichkeit* refers to the pull of physical desires and worldly impulses that challenge one’s moral resolve</term>, which is still so often superior to him in the struggle. The silent observer of nature weeps them in holy solitude over the general vanity and frailty of things. The philanthropist <note>original: *Menschenfreund*, literally a "friend of humanity."</note> weeps them over the great misery felt by thousands of his brothers. The admirer of genuine morality weeps them over the wretched delusion of the wicked. All these tears are also a kind gift from the Divinity, and grant to the noble heart a greater

### 18.

## Jesus towards the Abandoned, Unfortunate, and Sorrowful.

---

We are currently considering Jesus from a perspective that has already been generally admired in him. Nevertheless, let us take our own path, and in Jesus's **active love for humanity** <term>active love for humanity: translating *thätigen Menschenliebe*; a core Enlightenment concept (philanthropy) emphasizing practical, moral action over abstract dogma</term>, look more at the manner and way in which he expressed it than at the specific objects of it. First of all, let a few stories follow here—among many, the most remarkable for our point of view—from which the spirit of the man will afterwards be best recognized.

Jesus passes through a market town <note>original: *Marktflecken*; refers to a small town with the right to hold a market, but not a full city</note>, and here ten leprous men meet him, lift up their voices, and cry: "Jesus, dear Master! have mercy on us." He sees them and says: "Go and show yourselves to the priests." — They go there and are

---

Here we see a friend of humanity who makes the most noble use of his powers. Every unfortunate person is saved by him, every sorrowful person comforted, and the abandoned helped, without him taking heed of national differences or the false judgments of men. Depending on the circumstances, he either waits to be asked for help or offers it of his own accord, always prompted by his own heart.

He only finds satisfaction when his love can be active. But it is not the pursuit of vain human glory that moves him to become the savior of the unfortunate, the comforter of the sorrowful, and the helper of the abandoned. No, they must all praise God for what has happened to them. Nevertheless, they should also feel that they owe the benefit <note>The original German "Wohlthat" implies a kind act, a blessing, or a charitable deed bestowed upon someone.</note> to him. Hence the various signs by which the suffering are brought to this feeling. Hence the common words: “your

"faith has helped you." What intention does Jesus have in this—he who so often, out of noble modesty, commands that his benefits <note>The German "Wohlthaten" implies acts of kindness, blessings, or charitable deeds.</note> be kept secret? — The people are meant to reach the conviction that they can also be helped morally by the very same benefactor who has stilled their outward suffering. This is the true point of view from which all of Jesus’s benefits appear in the most charming light. The physical need is to be transformed into a moral one, and the physical benefit into a moral benefit. To that end, he uses every single circumstance with uncommon wisdom. Sometimes the eyewitnesses of the act learn that one does not place their trust in the messenger of the Godhead in vain; sometimes, that bringing joy to people on the Sabbath is the best form of worship <note>original: "Gottesverehrung"</note>; sometimes, that man has a sublime privilege over all other creatures of the earth; sometimes, that the charitable use of all one’s powers must not be restricted by any human commandments. What important moral truths for every eyewitness blinded by prejudice! On the other hand, those who see themselves freed from their evil <note>In this context, "Uebel" refers to their physical affliction or illness.</note> by Jesus are guided and encouraged toward the grateful adoration of the Godhead and—

And these are surely the most beautiful benefits <note>The German "Wohlthaten" refers to acts of kindness, blessings, or charitable deeds.</note> which simultaneously promote a moral purpose. Jesus always did good in this way, and we must remark on this occasion how strikingly he agrees in this with the Ruler of the world <note>Referring to God as the divine governor of the universe.</note>. Under His guidance, all the particular fates of man are meant to bring about a moral good, and all His providences <note>original: "Fügungen"</note> are calculated to ennoble humans from one side or another. Everyone who pays attention to this will know it from their own experience, and whoever examines history will be further strengthened in this experience. **O n e** can therefore say that all doing of good is only then a divine doing of good when a moral good is simultaneously intended and promoted in the world through it. From this perspective, the spirit of our Jesus also distinguishes itself chiefly. The following story will make us quite precisely acquainted with him in this regard. ### 19.

## Jesus's Judgment on Misfortune.

---

A man who treated the unfortunate in the most noble manner could have no false conceptions regarding the misfortune in the world.

A sound understanding has just as great an influence on the heart as a good heart has on the understanding. — The Roman <term>procurator</term> <note>A title for a Roman governor; in this case, Pontius Pilate, who ruled Judea.</note> Pilate had ordered a multitude of Galileans to be cut down during their sacrifice in the temple, presumably to prevent a rebellion through such a terrible example. In this instance, the innocent suffered along with the guilty — a proof that such a proceeding in no way accords with the laws of justice. People came to Jesus and brought him the sad news of the misfortune of his countrymen <margin>a) Luke 13:1.</margin>. Then he began, according to his custom—

<margin>a) Luke 13:1.</margin>

How humanely and with what gentle love does Jesus judge human fates here!

But how do we so often judge? — One finds it so easy to see in the misfortune of a neighbor the punishment for their transgressions, and hardly hesitates to say so aloud. Must this not weigh down the unfortunate person, if they are still alive, more than their actual condition, and discourage them more than their own situation? And from what does every inhumanity of this kind arise? Primarily from error and superstition. The old idea that the Deity is angry and reveals its anger through adverse fates over mankind has become almost a universal prejudice of all ages. To this—

> “This man is atoning neither for his own sin, nor for the sin of his parents. Rather, he is blind so that the works of God may be revealed in him.”

Should we express this answer in other words, it would sound something like this: it is impossible for the man born blind to atone for his own sins, for otherwise he would have had to have sinned while still in his mother’s womb; and he atones even less for the sins of his parents, for he would then be held guilty without having committed any fault. ## 20.

# Jesus Knows Mankind.

What is wisdom, what is virtue, without **knowledge of human nature** <term>knowledge of human nature</term> <note>Original: *Menschenkenntniß*. In the 18th and 19th centuries, this was considered a vital practical skill—the ability to discern a person's true character and motives beneath their outward appearance.</note>? Even if they are the daughters of heaven, knowledge of human nature must be in their friendship, or at least in their service, as long as they dwell on earth.

**Vice** <term>vice</term> <note>Original: *Laster*. The opposite of virtue; habitual moral failings.</note> gladly hides behind outwardly shining masks, assumes manifold forms, and seeks to reach its goal by hidden paths. The human heart, so capable of all **arts of disguise** <term>arts of disguise</term> <note>Original: *Verstellungskünste*. This refers to dissimulation or the act of pretending to be more virtuous than one actually is.</note> and intrigue, easily wins over the intellect to its advantage, and makes use of deceptive excuses to justify its will. The diversity of human characters, the influence of secret inclinations, the power of habit, the continuous play of passions, and the strength of deep-rooted prejudices on the one hand; original dispositions, fundamental drives and powers...

Everything by which people can usually be touched, moved, or guided is utilized within it with the highest wisdom. Therefore, he could rightly claim of a teaching that is perfectly suited for all social classes, circumstances, connections, ways of life, state institutions, and civil societies:

> "Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not." <note>A famous quotation from the Gospels (Matthew 24:35), here used to argue that Jesus’s insights are as permanent as the laws of nature.</note>

The very nature of humanity would first have to change before a religion firmly grounded upon this nature could become invalid. In exactly this way, the Deity educates the human race.

Cheerful children rejoice happily, passing their days in play; amidst constant pleasures and undisturbed by fear of the future or the pain that waits for them in the womb <note>Original: *Schoos*. Childhood rejoicing turns into seriousness, laughter into reflection, and loud play into quiet industriousness.
